One could consider such an application to be artificial and far-fetched; but it is important that it is _possible_.

### <span class="fac">[162\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/162.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">824.</span> If there is a temptation to view differential calculus as a calculus with infinitesimally small quantities, then it is understandable that in another case, an analogous temptation may be even more powerful – if, namely, it is thoroughly nourished by our forms of language; and one can imagine that it becomes irresistible.

### <span class="fac">[162\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/162.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">825.</span> “I had toothache” – when I say that, I do not remember my behaviour, but my pain. And how does that happen? Does one have a dim image of the pain floating before one? – Is it as if one had _very_ weak pain? “No; it is a different kind of image; something specific.” So is it as if someone had never seen a painted picture, but only busts, and one said to him, “No, a painting is quite different from a bust; it is a completely different kind of image.” It would perhaps be possible that one would find it much more difficult to explain what a painting is to a blind person than what a bust is.

### <span class="fac">[162\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/162.webp),[163\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/163.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">826.</span> But the word “specific” (or an analogous word) that one likes to use here does not help. It is as little an aid to understanding as the word “undefinable” when someone says that the property “good” is indefinable. What we want to overlook is the use of the word “good”, and also the use of the word “remember”. For one cannot say: “You _know_ the specific memory-image.” I do not _know_ it. – I can, of course, say: “I cannot describe Mr. N., but I know him”; but that means that I recognize him, not that I believe that I can recognize him again.

### <span class="fac">[163\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/163.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">827.</span> That it makes sense to say that someone had a feeling without ever communicating it, is connected with the fact that it makes sense to say: “I felt that at the time; I remember it.” One could explain the connection as follows: One wouldn’t say: “If I had never said that I was in pain at the time, I wouldn’t have been in pain either.”

### <span class="fac">[163\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/163.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">828.</span> “I know what it _means_ ‘He is in pain.’” Does that mean that I can _imagine_ it? And what is the importance of imagining? That I can, at any time, refer to my own pain in order to explain this sentence, or that I can proceed to cause myself pain now, etc., is certainly important.

### <span class="fac">[163\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/163.webp),[164\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/164.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">829.</span> How does one learn to call a piece of sugar “sugar”? How, by following the instruction “Give me a piece of sugar”? How, by uttering the words “Please ask for a piece of sugar” – that is, by expressing the wish?! How, by understanding the command “Throw!”; and how by understanding the expression of _intention_ “I will throw now”? Presumably, the adults show the child, they pronounce the word and then throw it – but now the child must _imitate_ that. (“But that is only the expression of intention if the child really has the intention in mind.” – But when does one say that this is the case?) And how does it learn to use the expression “I was about to throw”? And how does one know that it was really in that state of mind that I call “being about to ‥‥”? After having been taught the language-games, it uses the words in the appropriate situations, the words that the adults used in such cases, or it uses a more primitive expression that contains the essential _relationships_ to what was learned earlier, and the adults replace the more primitive expression with the correct expression.

### <span class="fac">[164\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/164.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">830.</span> The new (spontaneous, ‘specific’) is a language-game.

### <span class="fac">[164\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/164.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">831.</span> “But did all these phenomena – of pain, of wish, of intention, of memory, etc. – not exist before there was language?” – What is the _phenomenon_ of pain? – “What is a table?” – “Well, _that_ for example!” And that is indeed an explanation; but what it teaches is the technique of using the word “table.” And now the question is: Which explanation corresponds to it in the case of a ‘phenomenon’ of mental life? Well, there is no explanation here that one can recognize as the analogous one without further ado.

### <span class="fac">[164\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/164.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">832.</span> One can ask: Does something always float before my eyes when I understand a word?! (Similarly: “Does recognition always take place when I look at a well-known object?”)

### <span class="fac">[164\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/164.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">833.</span> But there is the phenomenon that a word, detached from any context – for example – has one meaning for a fleeting moment, but the next moment a different meaning; that if one pronounces the word several times in succession, it loses every ‘meaning’; and so on. And here it is a matter of a _floating_ thought.

### <span class="fac">[164\[6\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/164.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">834.</span> What would we say about a person who did not understand the words “I see this figure now as ‥‥, now as ‥‥”? Would something essential be missing; is it similar to being blind; or colorblind, or tone-deaf?

### <span class="fac">[164\[7\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/164.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">835.</span> Well, it is easy to imagine people who cannot ‘phrase’ drawings in this or that way; but would they not nevertheless see a drawing as this, and at another time as something else? Or should I assume that in this case they would not say that the visual image has remained essentially the same? Would they, for example, when the schematic representation of a cube appears to them now one way, now another, believe that the lines have changed their position?

### <span class="fac">[164\[8\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/164.webp),[165\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/165.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">836.</span> Imagine someone who dislikes looking at a drawing or photograph because he says that a colorless person is ugly. Or perhaps someone might find that tiny people, houses, etc., as they appear in pictures, are eerie or ridiculous. This would certainly be very strange behaviour. (‘You shall not make for yourself an image.’) Think of our reaction to a good photograph, to the facial expression of the photograph. There might be people who see in a photograph at most a kind of diagram, as we look at a map; we can extract various things from it about the landscape, but not, for example, admire the landscape while looking at the map, or exclaim “What a magnificent view!” The ‘shape-blind’ person must be abnormal in _this_ way.

### <span class="fac">[165\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/165.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">837.</span> How can the absence of an experience when hearing a word hinder or influence _calculation_ with words?

### <span class="fac">[165\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/165.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">838.</span> Imagine people who only think aloud and only imagine while drawing. Or perhaps it would be more correct to say: they draw where we imagine something. The case where I imagine my friend N does not correspond to the case where the other person draws him; rather, he must draw him and say, or write, that that is his friend N. – But if he has two friends who are similar and have the same name, and I ask him “Which one did you mean; the clever one, or the stupid one?” – he might not be able to answer that. But he could answer the question “Which of them is this representing?” – In this case, the answer is simply another use of the picture, not a statement about an experience.

### <span class="fac">[165\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/165.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">839.</span> Compare James’ idea that the thought is already complete at the beginning of the sentence with the idea of the lightning speed of thought and the concept of _intention_ to say this or that. The thought is already complete at the beginning of the sentence (and why not at the beginning of the ensuing one?) _means_ the same as: If someone is interrupted after the first word, and you then ask him “What did you want to say then?”, he can – at least often – answer the question. But here too, James says something that sounds like a psychological statement but is not one. Because whether the thought was already complete at the beginning of the sentence should be _proved_ by the experience of individual people.

### <span class="fac">[166\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/166.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">840.</span> Now, however, we can often not answer the question of what we wanted to say at the time. But in this case, we say that we have _forgotten_ it. Would it be conceivable that people in such cases would answer: “I only said _these_ words; how am I supposed to know what would have come next?” –

### <span class="fac">[166\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/166.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">841.</span> Whoever says “When I heard the word, it meant to me ‥‥” refers to _a point in time_ and _to a use of the word_. – The strange thing about it is, of course, the reference to the point in time. _This_ is what the ‘meaning-blind’ person would lose.

### <span class="fac">[166\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/166.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">842.</span> And whoever says “I wanted to continue at the time ‥‥” – refers to _a point in time_ and to an _action_.

### <span class="fac">[166\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/166.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">843.</span> When I speak of the essential _relations_ of the utterance, this happens because, in this way, the particularly essential _expressions_ of our language are _pushed into the background_. And the relations are essential to the utterance if they would lead us to translate an expression that is otherwise unfamiliar to us into the customary one.

### <span class="fac">[166\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/166.webp),[167\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/167.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">844.</span> How is it possible that someone never says, “At that time, I wanted to do this,” and that one couldn’t teach him to use such an expression? It is clear that someone can think a lot without thinking _that_. He can master a large area of language without mastering it. I mean that he remembers his utterances, including, for example, what he said to himself. So, he will say, for example, “I said to myself, ‘I want to go there,’ perhaps even, ‘I imagined the house and walked the path that leads to it.’” The characteristic thing here is that he has his intentions in the form of thoughts or images, and therefore they could always be replaced by uttering a sentence or seeing an image. He lacks the ‘lightning-fast’ quality of thought. – But should this mean that he often behaves like an automaton, walking down the street and doing errands; and if one meets him and asks, “Where are you going?” that he stares at one as if he had been sleepwalking? – He will not answer, “I don’t know.” Or will his actions seem aimless to him, or to us? I don’t see why! If I go to the baker, I might say to myself, “I need bread,” and walk the usual way. If one asks him, “Where are you going?” I want to assume that he answers with the expression of intention, as we do. – But will he also say, “When I left the house, I wanted to go to the baker, but now…?” No; but should we say that he therefore set out on his way as if sleepwalking?

### <span class="fac">[167\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/167.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">845.</span> But isn’t it strange that we don’t encounter such people, given the great variety of people? Or are these people found among the mentally deficient, and it is only not observed sufficiently which language-games they are capable of and which not?

### <span class="fac">[167\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/167.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">846.</span> Plato says that thinking is a conversation. If it were really a conversation, one could only report the words of the conversation and the external circumstances under which it was conducted, but not also the opinion that these words had for the speaker at that time. If someone said to himself (or aloud), “I hope to see N soon,” it would make no sense to ask, “And which person with that name did you have in mind at that time?” He just said those words. But couldn’t I imagine that he still wants to _continue_ in a certain way; so that I can ask him, “And do you have someone with that name in mind now, and who?” And assuming that he can usually continue, explain his words – what would be the difference between him and us? – He could report every thought literally. So, if he says, “I was just thinking of N,” and we ask him, “_How_ were you thinking of him?” he can always answer, unless he says he has forgotten.

### <span class="fac">[167\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/167.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">847.</span> If someone says to me, “N wrote to me,” I can ask, “Which N do you mean?” – And must he, in order to answer me, refer to an experience when uttering the name? – And if he now just utters the name N – perhaps as an introduction to a statement about N – can I not ask him in the same way, “Who do you mean?” and he answer in the same way?

### <span class="fac">[167\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/167.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">848.</span> One often just utters the name of a person, perhaps in a sigh. And the other person asks, “Who did you mean?” And how will our ‘meaning-blind’ person act? Will he not sigh, or be unable to answer the question, or answer, “I mean…,” instead of “I meant…?”

### <span class="fac">[168\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/168.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">849.</span> Imagine one of your acquaintances! Now say, who was it! – Sometimes the image comes first and the name later. But does this mean that I guess the name based on the similarity of the image? – And if the name only follows later, should I say that the image of the acquaintance was already there with the image, or was it only complete with the name? After all, I didn't infer the name from the similarity of the image; and that is precisely why I can say that the image was already there with the image.

### <span class="fac">[168\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/168.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">850.</span> “I must go to the bank and get money.” – How did you understand this sentence? Must this question mean something other than: “How would you explain this sentence, what action do you expect _from it_, etc.”? If the sentence is uttered under different circumstances, so that the word “bank” obviously means _that_ in one case, and something else in another, – must something special happen when hearing the sentence in order for you to understand it? Are not all _experiences_ of understanding covered up by the use, by the practice of the language-game? And that simply means: such experiences are of no interest to us here.

### <span class="fac">[168\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/168.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">851.</span> When I see the milkman coming, I get my jug and go to meet him. Do I experience an intention? Not that I know. (Perhaps as little as I try to go in order to go.) But if I am stopped and asked, “Where did you want to go with the jug?”, I would express my intention.

### <span class="fac">[168\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/168.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">852.</span> If I now say, for example, “I got up to go to the milk cart,” – should one call that the description of an experience of intending? And why is that misleading? Because there was no ‘expression’ of an experience here?

### <span class="fac">[168\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/168.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">853.</span> But if I say, “I got up to… then I thought again and…” – where is the experience here, and when did it happen? Was the experience only the ‘thinking again’, ‘deciding differently’?

### <span class="fac">[168\[6\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/168.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">854.</span> I take the milk jug, walk a few steps, then see that it is not clean, say “No!” and go to the tap. Then I describe what happened and _state_ my intentions. _Did_ I not have them? Of course! But again: is it not misleading to call them “experiences”? If one also calls what I said to myself, etc., in the same way! (It would also be misleading to call intention a “feeling”.)

### <span class="fac">[169\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/169.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">855.</span> And it is now the question whether, for the same reason, it was not completely misleading to speak of ‘blindness to form’ or ‘blindness to meaning’ (so one would speak of ‘blindness to will’ when someone behaves passively). For blind is precisely the one who does not have a sensation. (One cannot compare the mentally deficient – for example – with the blind.)

### <span class="fac">[169\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/169.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">856.</span> When I drew the first 🖵, it was half a circle, the second was half an S-line; the third was a whole.

### <span class="fac">[169\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/169.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">857.</span> “I have no doubt that this often happens.” – If you say that in a conversation, can you really believe that you are distinguishing between the meanings of the words ‘that’ and ‘this’ while speaking?

### <span class="fac">[169\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/169.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">858.</span> Against the fiction of people who can only think aloud, one might want to raise this objection: Suppose such a person says, “When I left the house, I said to myself, ‘I must go to the baker’s’” – couldn’t one ask him: “Did you really _mean_ these words? You could also have said them as a language exercise, or as a quotation, or for fun, or to mislead someone.” – That is true. But did what he did lie in the experience that accompanied the words? What supports such an assertion? Presumably, the fact that the person asked can answer, “I meant the sentence _in this way_,” without inferring this from _external circumstances_.

### <span class="fac">[169\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/169.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">859.</span> One will certainly say that whoever remembers having _meant_ these words remembers the experience of a certain _depth_, of a resonance. (If he hadn't meant them, he wouldn't have _had_ this resonance.) But isn't this just an illusion (similar to when someone believes they feel their thinking in their head)? One creates a _picture_ of the _processes_ by means of unsuitable concepts. (Cf. James.)

### <span class="fac">[169\[6\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/169.webp),[170\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/170.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">860.</span> Try _this_ experiment: Say a word with multiple meanings to yourself (“but”). Now, if you experience it as a verb, try to hold onto this experience, so that it continues. If you repeat the word to yourself often, it loses its meaning for you; and now ask yourself whether, when you use the word as a verb in ordinary speech, the word doesn't perhaps feel the same as when it lost its meaning through repeated use. You certainly cannot testify to the contrary from memory. Rather, one only finds that it could not be otherwise a priori.

### <span class="fac">[170\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/170.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">861.</span> It is quite irrelevant whether one says that one projects the interpretation of “but” onto the experience during the utterance later. For there is no difference here between projecting and describing.

### <span class="fac">[170\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/170.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">862.</span> One can take a drawing to be a real cube; but also, in the same sense, a triangle as lying or standing? – “As I got closer, I saw that it was only a drawing.” But not: “As I looked more closely, I saw that _this_ was the base and _this_ the apex.”

### <span class="fac">[170\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/170.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">863.</span> My words, “When you started to speak, I thought you meant…,” refer to the beginning of his speech and to an image that I had at the time. And it is of course possible that someone never _does_ such a thing. But I assume that he can answer the question, “Of which N was I talking?” at the end. And it is of course possible that he would have answered it differently if I had asked the question after the first words of my narration. So should he not understand the question: “Did you know from the beginning who I was talking about?” And if he does not understand such a question, will we not simply consider him mentally deficient? I mean: will we not simply assume that his thinking is not quite _clear_, or that he no longer remembers what he was thinking at the time – if he was thinking anything at all? That is, we will usually use a different image here than the one I propose.

### <span class="fac">[170\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/170.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">864.</span> But it is true: with mentally deficient people, we often have the feeling that they speak more automatically than we do. And if someone were what we call ‘meaning-blind,’ we would imagine that he must make a less vivid impression than we do, act more ‘like a machine.’ (One also says: “God knows what is going on in his mind!” and thinks of something vague, something disorderly.)

### <span class="fac">[170\[6\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/170.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">865.</span> It could be that when people are told an isolated word, they immediately form some sentence with that word, and that others do not; that the former is a sign of intelligence, the latter of dullness.

### <span class="fac">[170\[7\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/170.webp),[171\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/171.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">866.</span> What can be objected to the expression “specific psychological phenomenon,” or “irreducible phenomenon”? They are misleading; but where do they come from? One wants to say: “One cannot make comprehensible to someone who doesn't know sweet, bitter, red, green, sounds, and pain what these words mean.” But one can explain to someone who has not yet eaten a sour apple what is meant. Red is just _this_, and bitter _this_, and pain _this_. But if one says that, one must really demonstrate what these words mean; i.e., show something red, let someone taste something bitter, or let them taste it, inflict pain on oneself or the other person, etc. _Don't think that one can privately point to the pain within oneself._ But how will one then _demonstrate_ what “to imagine,” “to remember,” “to intend,” “to believe” means? The expression “specific psychological phenomenon,” however, corresponds to that of the private, indicative definition.

### <span class="fac">[171\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/171.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">867.</span> Is this (in the _end_) an illusion, if I believed that the other person's words had this sense for me at the time? Of course not! No more than it is an illusion to believe that one dreamed before waking up.

### <span class="fac">[171\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/171.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">868.</span> When I assumed the case of a ‘meaning-blind’ person, it was because the experience of meaning in the _use_ of language does not seem to have any importance. Because it thus seems as if not much can be lost to the meaning-blind person. However, this conflicts with the fact that we sometimes say that in a communication, a word meant _one_ thing to us until we saw that it meant something else. First, however, we do not feel in this case that the experience of meaning took place when _hearing the word_. Second, one could _rather_ speak here of an experience of the sense of the sentence, than of that of a word-meaning.

### <span class="fac">[171\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/171.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">869.</span> The picture that one connects, for example, with uttering the sentence “the bank is far away,” is now an illustration to _it_ and not to one of its words.

### <span class="fac">[171\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/171.webp),[172\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/172.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">870.</span> If someone insisted that he mostly experiences nothing at all when he hears a command, a communication, etc., at least nothing that determines the sense of the words for him, – could he not, in some form, say that he had understood the first words of the sentence _in this way_ and later changed his understanding? – But what _purpose_ would he have in saying that? It could explain a certain reaction _on his part_. He hears, for example, that N has died, and believes that his friend N is meant; then he realizes that this is not the case. He first looks dismayed; then relieved. – And it is easy to see what interest such an explanation can have.

### <span class="fac">[172\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/172.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">871.</span> What should I say now: – that the meaning-blind person is not able to react in that way? or that he simply does not claim that he experienced the meaning _at the time_, – that he therefore only uses a different picture?

### <span class="fac">[172\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/172.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">872.</span> Is the meaning-blind person therefore the one who does _not_ say: “The whole train of thought suddenly occurred to me”? Does this mean that he cannot say “Now I've got it!” –

### <span class="fac">[172\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/172.webp),[173\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/173.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">873.</span> “There was no tree and no bush there” – how does this sentence work? Well, “tree” stands for a thing that looks _like this_. Certainly: a tree looks like that; but is the idea of representing the thing by the word really so easy to understand? If I plan a garden, I can let a tree be represented there by a stake. Where the stake now stands, the tree will later be planted. – But one could still say that the word “tree” in the sentence represents the image of a tree (and as such, a tree can of course also be used). Because in place of the word “tree” one could put the image in a picture language, and the word “tree” is in any case connected with the image by the ostensive definition. Then it is the ostensive definition that determines what the word ‘_represents_’. And now apply this to the word “pain”, for example. – But doesn’t the sign 🖵 represent a house on a map? But only insofar as a house could also serve as a _sign_! But the sign does not represent the house for what it stands. – “Well, it _corresponds_ to it.” – If I go with the map in my hand and come to this house, I point to the place on the map and say “_This_ is the house”. – “The sign represents the house” would mean: “Because I cannot put the house itself in the map, I put this sign in its place.” But what would the house itself do in the map! A representation is something provisional, but if the sign _corresponds_ to the house, then there is nothing provisional here; it will not, when we come to the house, be replaced by the house. And since the sign is never replaced by its bearer, one could ask: How can an ink stroke replace a house? No: the stake replaces the tree, the picture can replace the person if one would rather see him, but has to be content with the picture; but even the sign on the map does not replace the object that it signifies.

### <span class="fac">[173\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/173.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">874.</span> When I am writing, do I feel something in my hand, or in my wrist? In general, no. But wouldn’t it feel different if my hand were anesthetized? Yes. And is this now proof that I _nevertheless_ feel something when I normally move my hand? I believe: _no_.

### <span class="fac">[173\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/173.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">875.</span> “I give you my full trust.” If the person who says this pauses after the word “you,” I may be able to continue; the situation provides what he wants to say. But if he unexpectedly continues “a gold watch” and I say “I was expecting something else” – does this mean that I experienced something during his first words, which one could call this understanding of the words? I don’t think one can say that.

### <span class="fac">[173\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/173.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">876.</span> Or imagine this conversation: He: “I give you –” I: “I know. But in this case, you don’t trust me _at all_.” – I interrupted him because I knew what he wanted to say. But did I necessarily complete the continuation in my thoughts? (Do I complete a sketch in my imagination?)

### <span class="fac">[173\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/173.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">877.</span> “I found myself going ‥‥” “saying ‥‥” etc.. This description does not _always_ apply when I say something, take a step, etc.

### <span class="fac">[173\[6\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/173.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">878.</span> Introspection can never lead to a definition. It can only lead to a psychological statement about the person who is introspecting. If someone says, for example, “When I hear a word, I believe that I always feel something, which I do not feel when I do not understand the word,” this is a statement about _his_ particular experiences. Another person may experience something completely different; and if both use the word “understand” correctly, then the essence of understanding lies in this use, and not in what they can say about their experiences.

### <span class="fac">[173\[7\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/173.webp),[174\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/174.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">879.</span> How would one describe someone who cannot understand the concept of ‘God,’ who cannot see how a reasonable person can use this word seriously? Should we say that he _suffers from a kind of blindness_?

### <span class="fac">[174\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/174.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">880.</span> One suddenly understands, suddenly repeats a word that the other person has said. He says to me, “It is seven o’clock”; I do not react at first; suddenly I exclaim, “Seven o’clock! I am already late ‥‥” It only then dawned on me what he had said. But what happened when I repeated the words “seven o’clock”? I cannot give any answer to this that would be _of interest_. Only again: I had only understood what he said, and so on; and that does not get us any further. This “only again” is, of course, what lies behind the talk of a ‘specific process’. (The absent-minded person, who, when given the command “Turn right!” turns left ‥‥․)

### <span class="fac">[174\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/174.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">881.</span> Does something happen when I understand the word, when I _intend this and that_? Does nothing happen? – But in what way is what happens _interesting_?!

### <span class="fac">[174\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/174.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">882.</span> An imbecile certainly would _not_ say: “When you started talking, I thought you meant ‥‥” – Now one might ask: is that because he always understands correctly from the start? Or because he never corrects himself? Or is what happens in him also happening in me, and he can just not express it?

### <span class="fac">[174\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/174.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">883.</span> “When you started talking, I thought you meant ‥‥ That is why I also made that movement ‥‥” So one explains what one did with the thought one had at the time. Do I really only come up with this explanation afterwards? Did I not really make that movement because I thought ‥‥? – – What kind of question is that? The “because” does not refer to a cause.

### <span class="fac">[174\[6\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/174.webp),[175\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/175.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">884.</span> “I will explain to you why I got up; I thought you meant ‥‥” – Yes, now I understand! – But what is the importance of this understanding? Well, for example: if the explanation had been different, then I would now have to react differently with words or actions. His thought is, in that sense, _like_ an action, or a process in his body. The account of his thought, as is the account of such processes. – – What interest do the words “I thought at first that you meant ‥‥” have? Often none at all. One could say that they reveal his world of thoughts to us. But what is the purpose of _that_? Why is this revelation not mere empty talk or mere fantasy?

### <span class="fac">[175\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/175.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">885.</span> One could (of course) call the account of such a perception an account of a _tendency_. (James). But here one must not see the experience of a tendency as the experience of something that is not quite complete! As if the experiences gave a colored picture, and some colors on it were applied in their full strength, while others are only hinted at, i.e., applied much more delicately. In itself, however, a delicate color is not the hint of a stronger one.

### <span class="fac">[175\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/175.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">886.</span> An event leaves a trace in memory: one sometimes thinks of it as if it consisted in the fact that it leaves a trace, an impression, a consequence in the nervous system. As if one could say: the nerves also have a memory. But if someone now remembers an event, then he must deduce it from this impression, this trace. Whatever the event leaves in the organism, it is not the memory. The organism compared to a dictaphone roll; the impression, the trace, is the change that the voice leaves on the roll. Can one say that the dictaphone (or the roll) remembers the spoken words when it reproduces them?

### <span class="fac">[175\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/175.webp),[176\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/176.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">887.</span> The feeling of dependence. How can one _feel_ that one is dependent? How can one _feel_: ‘It does not depend on me’. But what is that, anyway, this odd expression of a feeling? But if, for example, one had difficulty every morning in making certain movements, in raising one’s arm, and so on, and had to wait until the paralysis passed, and that sometimes took a long time, sometimes a short time, and one could not predict it and could not take any measure to speed it up – would that not give one a consciousness of dependence? Is it not the absence of the regular, or the vivid image of it, that underlies consciousness? It is, after all, the consciousness: “It shouldn’t be this way!” When I get up from the armchair, I usually do not say “Well, I can get up.” I might say it after an illness. But whoever said it _usually_, or whoever said afterwards “Well, it worked this time,” one could say that he has a special _attitude_ towards life.

### <span class="fac">[176\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/176.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">888.</span> Why does one say “He knows what he means”? How does one know that he _knows_ it? If he knows it, but I do not _know_ what he means – what would it be like if I knew it? Yes, if I knew it and he didn’t? How would someone have to behave so that we would say: “He _knows_ what the other person is experiencing”? But must there even be a case that we would describe in this way, consistently? It is not even clear that any phenomenon would have to be described with the words “A is in pain in B’s body”. That is to say: one can say “Wouldn’t that be a logical application of this expression?” but I may or may not be inclined to call it logical.

### <span class="fac">[176\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/176.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">889.</span> Remember especially the expression in the dream narrative: “And I knew that ‥‥”. One might think: It is rather strange that one can dream that one _knew_. One also says: “and I knew in the dream that ‥․”

### <span class="fac">[176\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/176.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">890.</span> Not everything I do, I do with an intention. (I whistle to myself, etc. etc..) But if I were to get up now and go out of the house, then come back in, and when asked “Why did you do that?” answered “For no particular reason,” or “Just like that,” that would seem strange, and someone who often did something like that without a particular intention would deviate greatly from the norm. Would that have to be what one calls “mentally deficient”?

### <span class="fac">[176\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/176.webp),[177\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/177.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">891.</span> Now imagine someone of whom one would say that he can never remember an intention except by remembering the utterance of an intention. Someone might do what we normally do ‘with a specific intention’, without such an intention, but it would nevertheless prove useful. And we might in such a case say that he acted with _unconscious_ intention. He suddenly climbs on a chair and then climbs down again. When asked “why” he has no answer; then, however, he reports that he noticed this and that from the chair, as if he had climbed up to observe it. Could not a ‘meaning-blind’ person behave similarly?

### <span class="fac">[177\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/177.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">892.</span> “When I said ‘He is an ass,’ I meant ‥‥” What kind of connection do those sounds have with that person? – Asked, “Who do you mean?” I will name him, describe him, show his photograph, etc. Is there any other connection? One that existed especially at the time of the utterance? But during the whole sentence, or only during the time I said “he”? No answer!

### <span class="fac">[177\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/177.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">893.</span> The experience during those words – one would like to say – naturally grows into this explanation.

### <span class="fac">[177\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/177.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">894.</span> But it is the case: I sometimes say, in conversation, “He is an ass,” and if I were asked, “Did you have a different experience during those words if we had spoken of N instead of M,” I would have to admit that this need not be the case. On the other hand, it sometimes seems to me as if I had an experience during the utterance that unambiguously belongs to _him_. The experiences during speaking seem to be clearly connected to _him_.

### <span class="fac">[177\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/177.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">895.</span> “Of course, I was thinking of him: I had him in front of me!” – but I didn’t recognize him from his picture.

### <span class="fac">[177\[6\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/177.webp),[178\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/178.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">896.</span> I suddenly say, “He is an ass.” A: “Who did you mean?” I: “N.” A: “Did you think of him while you said _the sentence_, or only when you gave the explanation?” – I could now answer that my words were the end of a longer train of thought. I had been thinking of N all along. And could I now say that the words themselves were not connected to him by any particular experience, but rather the entire train of thought? So, with those words, I could have just as easily meant someone else, and to whom they referred depended on what preceded them. But in order to say that I spoke of him, that I meant him, that I thought of him, must I be able to remember an experience that is necessarily connected to him? Could it not be that it always seems to me as if _nothing_ happened during my words that could only be interpreted as referring to him? So, I imagine that I am always _conscious_ that my mental images are ambiguous. But – I assume – I still say, “I meant the ‥‥.” But is this not a contradictory assumption? No; that is actually how it is. I say, “I meant the ‥‥”: _thus_ I continue.

### <span class="fac">[178\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/178.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">897.</span> I spoke to my neighbor about her doctor; at the same time, I had a picture of this person in mind – but I had never seen him, only knew his name, and perhaps I formed an image of him based on this. How can this image be characteristic of the fact that I am speaking of _him_? – And yet, it seemed to me that way, until I remembered that I don’t even know what this man looks like. His image represents him _to me_ no better than his name.

### <span class="fac">[178\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/178.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">898.</span> If I compare the occurrence of _meaning_ with a dream, then our speech is usually dreamless. The ‘meaning-blind’ person would be one who always speaks dreamlessly.

### <span class="fac">[178\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/178.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">899.</span> And one can indeed ask: What business do his dreams have with me? Why must I be interested in what he dreams and whether he dreams while he is speaking to me or listening to me? – This does not, of course, mean that these dreams can never interest me. But why should they be the most important thing in linguistic communication?

### <span class="fac">[178\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/178.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">900.</span> The use of the term ‘dream’ here is useful; but only if one sees that it still contains a flaw.

### <span class="fac">[178\[6\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/178.webp),[179\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/179.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">901.</span> “I thought all along that you were talking about ‥‥” – How _was_ it? – – But not differently than if he had actually been talking about that person. The fact that I later realize that I misunderstood him does not change what happened during the understanding. – So, if the sentence “I thought at the time that you meant ‥‥” is the report of a ‘dream,’ then that means that I _always_ ‘dream’ when I understand a sentence.

### <span class="fac">[179\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/179.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">902.</span> One also says: “I assumed that you were talking about ‥‥,” and that already sounds less like the report of an experience.

### <span class="fac">[179\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/179.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">903.</span> “I thought you were talking about ‥‥ and was surprised that you say ‥‥ about him” – This surprise is again in a similar case: Here, too, we have the feeling that one only completes the rudimentary experience with the _uttering_ of this thought.

### <span class="fac">[179\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/179.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">904.</span> Well, it is true! For sometimes, when I say “I thought ‥‥,” I can report that at the time I actually said these words aloud or to myself; or that at the time I did not use these, but other words, of which the present ones are a paraphrase. _That happens sometimes_! In contrast to this, however, is the case in which my present expression is not the _reproduction of something_. For it is only a ‘reproduction’ if it is a reproduction according to the rules of depiction.

### <span class="fac">[179\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/179.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">905.</span> Anyone who would not be able to say: the word “but” could be a verb or a conjunction, or to form sentences in which it is one or the other, could not manage simple school exercises. But _that_ is not what is required of a student; to grasp the word in a certain way outside of a context, or to report how he has grasped it.

### <span class="fac">[179\[6\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/179.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">906.</span> I would like to say: the conversation, the application, and the interpretation of the words flow along, and it is only in that flow that the word has its meaning. “He has left.” – “Why?” What did you mean when you uttered the word “Why?” What _did_ you think of?

### <span class="fac">[179\[7\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/179.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">907.</span> “I thought you meant _that_.” – Well, that is not the same as “I think you meant that.” Do not let the comparison with another use of the past tense _mislead_ you!

### <span class="fac">[179\[8\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/179.webp),[180\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/180.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">908.</span> We play this game: there are pictures and words are spoken, and we have to point to the picture that corresponds to the word. Among the words, there are also ambiguous ones. With the word ‥‥, only _one_ meaning occurs to me and I point to a picture; later, another occurs to me and I point to a different picture. Will the “blind person for meaning” be able to do this? Of course. – But how is it _with that_? A word is named, one of its meanings occurs to me. I do not say it, but I look for the picture. Before I have found it, another meaning of the word occurs to me; I say: “A second meaning has just occurred to me.” And then I explain: “First, _this_ meaning occurred to me, then _that_.” Can _the_ “blind person for meaning” do that? – Can he not say that he knows the meaning of the word, but he does not say it? Or can he not say that it has just _occurred_ to him, but he does not say it? – It seems to me that he can say both of those things. But then also: “When you said the word, _this_ meaning occurred to me.” And why not: “When I said the word, I first meant it in _this_ meaning”?

### <span class="fac">[180\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/180.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">909.</span> It is as if the word that I understand has a certain light aroma, which corresponds to the understanding. As if two words that are quite familiar to me differ not only in their sound or their appearance, but, even if I do not _associate anything with them_, also in an atmosphere. – But remember how the names of famous poets and composers seem to have acquired their own meaning. So that one can say: the names “Beethoven” and “Mozart” not only sound different, but they are also accompanied by a different _character_. If you were now to describe this character more closely, would you show their pictures or play their music? And now the “blind person for meaning”: He would not feel that the names differ in some way when heard or seen. And what would he have lost by that? – And yet, when he hears a name, first _one_ bearer and later another may occur to him. –

### <span class="fac">[180\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/180.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">910.</span> I said that the words “Now I can do it!” do not express an _experience_. Well, no more than “Now I will raise my arm.” – – But why do they not express an experience, a feeling? – How are they used? Both, for example, as an introduction to an action. The fact that a statement refers to a point in time, in which, however, nothing happens in the external world that it means, that of which it speaks, does not show us that it speaks of an experience.

### <span class="fac">[180\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/180.webp),[181\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/181.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">911.</span> Think of students ‘indicating’ when they know an answer. Did one have to have silently rehearsed the answer in order to be able to indicate it with sense? And _what_ must have preceded this in him? – Nothing. But it is important that he would usually give an answer when he has indicated; and that is the criterion for whether he understands the indicating.

### <span class="fac">[181\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/181.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">912.</span> “The words ‘the rose is red’ are senseless if the word ‘is’ has the meaning of ‘is equal to’.” We have the idea that whoever tried to pronounce the words “the rose is red” with these meanings would have to get stuck in his thinking. (Just as one cannot think a contradiction because the thought, as it were, breaks one.) One would like to say: “You cannot mean these words in this way and still connect a sense with the whole.”

### <span class="fac">[181\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/181.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">913.</span> Could one say that meaning-blindness manifests itself in the fact that one cannot successfully tell this person: “You must hear the word as ‥‥, then you will speak the sentence correctly.” This is the instruction that one gives someone when playing a piece of music. “Play it as if it were the answer” – and one makes a gesture for it. But how does one translate this gesture into the game? If he understands me, he now plays it according to my wish. But could you not also give such an instruction with the help of “louder,” “softer,” “faster,” “slower”? No; I could not. For if he now plays this tone louder, that one softer, I don’t even know it. Thus, I can also say “Make a mischievous face” and know if he has made one without being able to describe the geometric changes of the face beforehand or afterward.

### <span class="fac">[181\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/181.webp),[182\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/182.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">914.</span> If one asks, “Is the experience of a meaning analogous to the experience of a mental image,” does one mean: is the difference not simply that of another _content_? Well, what is the content of the experience of an image? “It is _this_” – but I must point to an image or a description. – “One experiences here and there” (one would like to say). “Only something else. Another content is presented to consciousness – stands before _it_.” And that is, of course, a very misleading picture. For it is the illustration of an idiom and explains nothing. Similarly, one could, in order to explain the chemical symbolism of a structural formula, devise pictures in which the elements are represented as people shaking hands. (Illustration of the alchemists).

### <span class="fac">[182\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/182.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">915.</span> If someone says that he had a mental image of a gold-shining sphere, we will understand that, but not if he says that this sphere was hollow. But in a dream, one could see a sphere and _know_ that it was hollow.

### <span class="fac">[182\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/182.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">916.</span> The instruction “As if from afar” in Schumann. Must everyone understand such an instruction? Everyone, for example, who would understand the instruction “Not too fast”? Is not the ability that is said to be lacking in the meaning-blind person of this kind?

### <span class="fac">[182\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/182.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">917.</span> Can one fix the understanding of a meaning, just as one can fix a mental image? So, if suddenly a meaning of the word occurs to me, can it also remain present before my mind?

### <span class="fac">[182\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/182.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">918.</span> “The whole plan stood before my mind in a flash and remained so for a minute.” One would like to say that what remained was not the same as what flashed. (Just as one cannot prolong a diphthong.)

### <span class="fac">[182\[6\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/182.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">919.</span> Namely, if this happened, that I said, “Now I’ve got it!” (i.e., the flash), then one cannot, of course, talk about it remaining.

### <span class="fac">[182\[7\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/182.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">920.</span> “Yes, I know the word. It’s on the tip of my tongue.” – Here, one is struck by the idea of the gap, about which James speaks, into which only this word fits, etc. – One somehow already experiences the word, even though it is not yet there. – – One experiences a _growing_ word. – And of course, I could also say that I experienced a growing meaning, or a growing explanation of the meaning. – It is only odd that we do not want to say that something was there, which then grew into this explanation. For if you ‘point out’, you say that you already know it. – Perhaps; but you could also say, “Now I can say it,” and whether this _ability_ grows into a saying, you do not know. And how about if one said: “The saying is then the fruit of _this_ ability, when it has grown out of this ability.”

### <span class="fac">[183\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/183.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">921.</span> When I wanted to say, when I could say, I didn’t actually _say_ it.

### <span class="fac">[183\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/183.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">922.</span> Of course, there is also something wrong with the explanation that the meaning or its explanation has grown out of a certain seed. In fact, we do not perceive such a growth; or only in very rare cases. And this explanation arises precisely from the tendency to explain, rather than merely to describe.

### <span class="fac">[183\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/183.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">923.</span> The mere description is so difficult because one believes one must supplement it to understand the facts. It is as if one sees a canvas with scattered patches of color, and says: as they are, they are incomprehensible; they only become meaningful if one supplements them to form a shape. – – Whereas I want to say: Here _is_ the whole. (If you supplement it, you distort it.)

### <span class="fac">[183\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/183.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">924.</span> Of course, the _meaning_ occurred to me then! Not at the time when I report it, nor in the meantime. That is precisely what one calls: that is precisely the use of the words “The meaning occurred to me.” (“in this so-called twentieth century”).

### <span class="fac">[183\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/183.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">925.</span> “The meaning is not something that one can _experience_!” – Why not? – The meaning is not a sense impression. But what are sense impressions? Something like a smell, a taste, a pain, a sound, etc. etc.. But what is ‘something like’ all these _things_? What do they have in common? This question is, of course, not to be answered by delving into these sense impressions. But one could ask: “Under what circumstances would we say that someone has a kind of sense impression that we lack?” – We say, for example, of animals that they have an organ with which they perceive this and that, and such a sensory organ does not have to be similar to one of ours.

### <span class="fac">[183\[6\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/183.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">926.</span> Could one imagine a sense perception by which we grasp the shape of a solid body, the _whole_ shape, not just what can be seen from _one_ point of view? Such a person would be able, for example, to model a body in clay without having to walk around it or touch it.

### <span class="fac">[183\[7\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/183.webp),[184\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/184.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">927.</span> Is it the diversity of possible explanations of a meaning that is at the bottom of the fact that one does not experience a meaning ‘in the same sense’ as a visual image?

### <span class="fac">[184\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/184.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">928.</span> What makes a mental image of him a mental image of _him_? – What makes his portrait a portrait of _him_? The painter’s intention? And does that mean: his state of mind? – And what makes a photograph a portrait of _him_? The photographer’s intention? And suppose a painter intends to draw N from memory, but, guided by forces in the unconscious, he draws an excellent portrait of M – would we then call it a bad portrait of N? And imagine people who are trained to draw portraits and ‘mechanically’ reproduce the person sitting in front of them. (Human copying machines). And now, what makes a mental image of him my mental image of _him_? – Nothing of what applies to the portrait applies to the mental image. The _question_ makes a mistake.

### <span class="fac">[184\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/184.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">929.</span> Whoever had the meaning in mind, and did not _forget_ it again, can now use the word in this way. Whoever had the meaning in mind, now _knows_ it, and the occurrence was simply the beginning of knowledge. Here there is no analogy with the experience of a mental image.

### <span class="fac">[184\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/184.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">930.</span> But what if I say to myself, I want to call _this_ (while, for example, looking at a particular figure) so and so (‘x’)? I can also say the ostensive definition “That is called ‘x’” aloud. But I must also understand it myself! I must therefore _know_ how, according to what technique, I intend to use the sign “x”. – If I am asked, “Do you also know _how_ you will use the word?”, I will answer: yes.

### <span class="fac">[184\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/184.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">931.</span> But what if religion teaches that the mind can exist even when the body has decayed? Do I understand what it teaches? Of course, I understand it – I can imagine many things about it. (People have also painted pictures of these things. And why should such a picture only be an imperfect representation of the expressed thought? Why shouldn’t it serve the _same_ purpose as what we say?) And what matters is the purpose.

### <span class="fac">[184\[6\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/184.webp),[185\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/185.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">932.</span> But aren’t you a pragmatist? No. Because I am not saying that the sentence is true if it is useful. The usefulness, i.e. the use, gives the sentence its special meaning; the language-game gives it meaning. And in so far as a rule is often given in such a way that it proves useful, and mathematical sentences are related to rules in their nature, usefulness is reflected in mathematical truths.

### <span class="fac">[185\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/185.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">933.</span> The soulful facial expression. One must remember that one can _paint_ a face with a soulful expression in order to believe that it is only colors that have this effect. It is unbelievable that it is only the _eyes_ – eyeball, eyelids, eyelashes, etc. – of a person that one can lose oneself in, that one can look at with wonder and delight. And yet, it is the eyes of a person that have this effect. “From which you can see ‥‥․”

### <span class="fac">[185\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/185.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">934.</span> Do I _believe_ in a mind in the other person when I look into his eyes with wonder and delight?

### <span class="fac">[185\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/185.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">935.</span> The sentence “if p, then q”, such as “if he comes, he will bring me something”, is not the same as “p ⊃ q”. Because the sentence “If ‥‥, then ‥‥” allows for the subjunctive, while the sentence “p ⊃ q” does not. – If someone answers the sentence “If he comes, ‥‥” with “That is not true”, he does not want to say: “He comes, and will bring nothing”, but: “He _may_ come and bring nothing”. From “p ⊃ q” it does not follow that “If p, then q”; because I can very well assert the first sentence (I know, for example, that <math class="stacked" display="inline"><mover><mrow><mi>p</mi></mrow><mo>‾</mo></mover><mspace width="0.3em"/><mtext>&</mtext><mover><mrow><mi>q</mi></mrow><mo>‾</mo></mover></math> is the case) and deny the second sentence.

### <span class="fac">[185\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/185.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">936.</span> Should I now say that the sentence “If ‥‥, then ‥‥” is either true, or false, or undecided? (Does the law of excluded third not apply?)

### <span class="fac">[185\[6\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/185.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">937.</span> One also gives the answer “Not necessarily” to the statement “If he comes, he will bring something.” – Also: “That does not follow.” – Russell said that when one asserts “If ‥‥, then ‥‥,” one usually means not the material but the formal implication; but that is also not correct. “If ‥‥, then ‥‥” cannot be reproduced in expressions of Russellian logic.

### <span class="fac">[185\[7\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/185.webp),[186\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/186.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">938.</span> One can very well say that the sentence “If ‥‥, then ‥‥” is either true, or it is false, or it is undecided. – But on what occasion would one say that? I think: as an introduction to further discussion. One discusses the matter _from these three perspectives_. I divide the field of possibilities into three parts. One might now say: a _sentence_ divides it into _two_ parts. But why? Unless that is part of the definition of a sentence. Why should I not also call something a sentence that makes a threefold division?

### <span class="fac">[186\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/186.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">939.</span> Now take a twofold division: I say: “Either he comes, or he does not come. – In the first case ‥‥ In the second ‥‥” _Can_ I not apply this way of considering to the sentence “If ‥‥ and ‥‥ meet, an explosion will occur”? If someone, for example, has made this assertion, can I not reply: “Either you are right in that, or you are not: if it is as you say, then ‥‥ if it is not so, then ‥‥”?

### <span class="fac">[186\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/186.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">940.</span> The law of excluded third does not say, as its form suggests: there are only the two possibilities, yes and no, and no third. But: “Yes” and “No” divide the field of possibilities into two parts. – And that does not necessarily have to be the case. (“Have you stopped beating your wife?”)

### <span class="fac">[186\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/186.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">941.</span> ‘A wish is a behaviour of the mind, of the soul, towards an object.’ ‘A wish is a state of mind that relates to an object.’ To make this more comprehensible, one thinks, for example, of longing and the fact that the object of our longing is before our eyes and we look at it longingly. If it is not before us, then its picture represents it, and if there is no picture, then an image. And so the wish is a behaviour of the soul towards an image. But one always thinks of a behaviour of the body towards an object. The behaviour of the soul towards the image is precisely what one could represent in a picture: the soul of the person, as it leans with a longing gesture towards the picture (the real picture) of an object.

### <span class="fac">[186\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/186.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">942.</span> And one could in this way also _represent_ how a person gives no expression to a wish in his facial expression, and yet his soul _longs for it_.

### <span class="fac">[187\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/187.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">943.</span> “The sentence ‘If only he would come!’ can be charged with our longing.” – With what was it charged? It is as if a weight from our mind were added to it. Yes, I would like to say all that. And is it irrelevant that I want to say it?

### <span class="fac">[187\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/187.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">944.</span> Is it irrelevant that I want to say it? Is it not important? Is it not important that hope _lives in my breast_? Is that not an image of some important human behaviour? Why does a person think that a thought comes into his head? – Or rather: he does not _believe_ it; he experiences it. Because he perhaps touches his head, closes his eyes in order to be alone with himself in his head. Leans his head back and makes a hand gesture to indicate that nothing should disturb the process in his head. – Well, are those not important types of behaviour?

### <span class="fac">[187\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/187.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">945.</span> And if the image of a thought in one’s head can force itself upon us, why then not even more so, the image of a thought in the mind?

### <span class="fac">[187\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/187.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">946.</span> What better image of faith could there be than a person, with an expression of faith, saying, “I believe…”?

### <span class="fac">[187\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/187.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">947.</span> Man is the best image of the human mind.

### <span class="fac">[187\[6\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/187.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">948.</span> It is of course important that one can easily represent the desire for an apple in a pictorial way, without putting words into the mouth of the person desiring it, – but that the conviction that something is so and so cannot be represented in the same way. _Important_, because it shows the difference, the difference in essence, between the psychological phenomena, and the way in which it can be described.

### <span class="fac">[187\[7\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/187.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">949.</span> Why did I say “difference in essence”? Is it a difference like that between carbon, gravity, the speed of light, and ultraviolet rays? Which are all ‘objects’ that natural science deals with. –

### <span class="fac">[187\[8\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/187.webp),[188\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/188.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">950.</span> Let’s think, we are talking about phenomena when people speak. It might be of interest to us: the speed of speaking, the change in intonation, the gestures, the length or shortness of the sentences, etc. etc. – Now, if one says that a person has a mental life: that he thinks, wishes, fears, believes, doubts, has images, is sad, cheerful, etc. – is that analogous to: he eats, drinks, speaks, writes, runs – or is it analogous to: he moves sometimes quickly, sometimes slowly, sometimes towards a goal, sometimes without a goal, sometimes steadily, sometimes jerkily?

### <span class="fac">[188\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/188.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">951.</span> Think of what one can call the character of a line, and of all that must be called a description of its character. What can one ask about, if one is interested in the character of a line?

### <span class="fac">[188\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/188.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">952.</span> Imagine, we are observing the movement of a point, for example a black point on a white surface. All sorts of important conclusions could be drawn from the character of this movement. But what can we all observe? – Whether the point moves uniformly or non-uniformly; whether its speed changes periodically; whether it changes steadily or abruptly; whether the point describes a closed line; how close it comes to a circle; whether the point describes a wave line and what its amplitude and wavelength are; and countless other things. And each of these facts could be the only thing that interests us. It could be, for example, that we are indifferent to everything else about this movement, except for the number of corners of the path in a certain time. And that means that if we are not only interested in _one_ property of this movement, but in many, each of them can give us a special, completely different insight. And so it is with the behaviour of people, with the various characteristics of this behaviour that we observe.

### <span class="fac">[188\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/188.webp),[189\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/189.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">953.</span> So, does psychology deal with behaviour, rather than with the mental states of a person? If one conducts a psychological experiment, what will one report? What the subject says, what it does, what happened to it in the past, & how it reacted. – & not: what the subject thinks, what it sees, feels, believes, experiences? – If one describes a painting, does one describe the arrangement of the brushstrokes on the canvas – & not what the viewer _sees_? But how is it with this: In the experiment, the observer will sometimes say: “The subject said ‘I feel …’, & I had the impression that this was true.” – Or one says: “The subject seemed tired.” Is this now a statement about its behaviour? One might say: Of course, what else could it be? – – One can also report: “The subject said ‘I am tired.’” – but for the evaluation of these words, it will be about whether they are credible, whether they were attributed to someone else, whether they were a translation from French, etc. Now, think about this: I say, “He made a distressed impression.” I am asked: “What was it that gave you this impression?” I say, “I don’t know.” – Can one now say that I have described his behaviour? Can one not say that I have described his face if I say “He had a sad face”? Even if I cannot indicate which spatial changes in the face made this impression? One might reply: “If you had looked more closely, you could describe the characteristic colours – & changes in location.” But who says that I, or anyone, could do that?

### <span class="fac">[189\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/189.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">954.</span> Once again: If I report “He was in a bad mood,” am I reporting a behaviour, or a state of mind? (If I say “The sky looks ominous,” am I talking about the present, or the future?) Both; but not side by side; but in one sense, one, and in another sense, the other. But what does that mean? (Is this not mythology? No.)

### <span class="fac">[189\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/189.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">955.</span> It is here just as with talking about physical objects and sense impressions. We have here two language-games, and their relationships to each other are complicated. If one wants to describe these relationships in a simple way, one will fail.

### <span class="fac">[189\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/189.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">956.</span> Imagine, I describe a psychological experiment: the apparatus, the questions of the experimenter, the answers and actions of the subject. And then I say: all of this is a scene in that and that play. Now everything has changed. One will therefore say: If this experiment were described in the same way in a book about psychology, then the description of the subject’s behaviour would be understood as an expression of the state of mind, because one assumes that the subject is telling the truth, is not trying to deceive us, has not learned the answers by heart. – So, are we making an assumption?

### <span class="fac">[189\[5\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/189.webp),[190\[1\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/190.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">957.</span> The nurse says to the doctor “He groans” – sometimes she wants to say “He is in great pain”; sometimes “He groans – even though he has nothing wrong with him”; sometimes “He groans; whether he is in pain, or merely makes this sound, I do not know.” Are we making an assumption? – We use the statement differently each time.

### <span class="fac">[190\[2\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/190.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">958.</span> “Of course, the psychologist reports the words, the behaviour of the subject, but only as signs of mental processes.” – That is correct. If the words and the behaviour, for example, have been learned, then they do not interest the psychologist. And yet the expression “as signs of mental processes” is misleading, because we are used to speaking of the colour of the face as a sign of fever. And every bad analogy is now explained with another bad analogy, so that we are finally relieved of the inconsistencies only by exhaustion.

### <span class="fac">[190\[3\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/190.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">959.</span> Imagine, one said: every word that is familiar to us already has a hazy area, a ‘court’ of faintly indicated uses surrounding it. As if, in a painting, the main figures are surrounded by delicate, misty images of events in which these figures have a share. – Well, let us simply take this assumption seriously! – Then it turns out that it is unable to explain the _intention_. For if it is the case that the possibilities of the use of an expression float before one in half-tones when listening or speaking, – if that is the case, then it applies to _us_. But we communicate with others without ever having asked them whether they also have these experiences.

### <span class="fac">[190\[4\]](https://cdn.wittgensteinnachlass.com/2000px/webp/Ts-245/190.webp)</span>

<span class="series-number">960.</span> And what about the constant becoming and passing away _in the realm_ of our consciousness? Well, what is it: is it an experience, or can one not imagine it any other way? There is an ambiguity here.
